---
title: A relation f(x/z, y/z) = 0 and the identity x z_x + y z_y = z
category: calculus/multivariable/diff-calculus/dc-implicit-function
level: l2
difficulty: 3
tags: implicit differentiation, homogeneous functions, chain rule, cone
order: 24
date: 2026-11-14
---

# Problem 24 — Homogeneous relations and Euler

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}$ be $C^1$ and suppose that $z=z(x,y)$ is defined implicitly by $f\left(\frac xz,\frac yz\right)=0$.

1. Show that $x\,z_x+y\,z_y=z$ (under a suitable hypothesis that you should state).
2. Check on $f(u,v)=u^2+v^2-1$, for which the relation is the cone $x^2+y^2=z^2$.
3. Interpret the identity geometrically.

## Hint

Let $F(x,y,z)=f(x/z,y/z)$. Use the chain rule to compute $F_x$, $F_y$, $F_z$; the combination $xF_x+yF_y+zF_z$ simplifies a lot.

## Solution

**Step 1: derivatives of $F$.** Write $f_1,f_2$ for the partial derivatives of $f$, evaluated at $(u,v)=(x/z,y/z)$. By the chain rule
$$F_x=\frac{f_1}z,\qquad F_y=\frac{f_2}z,\qquad F_z=-\frac{x}{z^2}f_1-\frac{y}{z^2}f_2=-\frac{xf_1+yf_2}{z^2}.$$

**Hypothesis.** To apply the implicit function theorem we need $F_z\ne0$, i.e. $xf_1+yf_2\ne0$ (and $z\ne0$). Then
$$z_x=-\frac{F_x}{F_z}=\frac{zf_1}{xf_1+yf_2},\qquad z_y=-\frac{F_y}{F_z}=\frac{zf_2}{xf_1+yf_2}.$$

**Step 2: the identity.**
$$xz_x+yz_y=\frac{z(xf_1+yf_2)}{xf_1+yf_2}=z.\ \blacksquare$$

**Step 3: the cone.** $f(u,v)=u^2+v^2-1$ gives $x^2+y^2=z^2$, i.e. $z=\sqrt{x^2+y^2}$ on the upper nappe, with $z_x=\dfrac xz$, $z_y=\dfrac yz$. Then $xz_x+yz_y=\dfrac{x^2+y^2}z=\dfrac{z^2}z=z$ ✓. The hypothesis: here $f_1=2u=2x/z$, $f_2=2v=2y/z$, so
$$xf_1+yf_2=\frac{2x^2}z+\frac{2y^2}z=\frac{2(x^2+y^2)}z=2z\ne0$$
for $z\ne0$ ✓.

**Geometric meaning.** The relation $f(x/z,y/z)=0$ is invariant under $(x,y,z)\mapsto(\lambda x,\lambda y,\lambda z)$: the surface is a **cone with vertex at the origin** (a union of lines through $0$). Hence $z(x,y)$ is homogeneous of degree $1$: $z(\lambda x,\lambda y)=\lambda z(x,y)$, and Euler's identity for homogeneous functions of degree $1$ reads precisely $xz_x+yz_y=z$.

**Common mistake.** Writing $F_z=-\frac xz f_1-\frac yzf_2$ (forgetting the factor $\frac1z$ from the derivative of $\frac1z$). The derivative of $x/z$ with respect to $z$ is $-x/z^2$, not $-x/z$.

**Remark.** This identity is a statement about the tangent plane: it says that the tangent plane at a point $P$ of a cone passes through the vertex. Indeed the tangent plane at $(x,y,z)$ is $Z-z=z_x(X-x)+z_y(Y-y)$, and at $(X,Y,Z)=(0,0,0)$ this reads $-z=-xz_x-yz_y$, which is exactly the identity.

---

---
title: Maximum and minimum of y on the ellipse x² − xy + y² = 3
category: calculus/multivariable/diff-calculus/dc-implicit-function
level: l2
difficulty: 2
tags: implicit function, critical points, second derivative test, conic
order: 25
date: 2026-11-14
---

# Problem 25 — Extrema of an implicit function

## Statement

Let $F(x,y)=x^2-xy+y^2-3$.

1. Show that wherever $F_y\ne0$ the curve $F=0$ defines $y=\varphi(x)$ locally, and that a critical point of $\varphi$ ($\varphi'=0$) must satisfy $F_x=0$, i.e. $y=2x$.
2. Find the critical points of $\varphi$ on the curve.
3. Using the second derivative of $\varphi$, determine their nature, and find the maximum and minimum of $y$ on the curve.

## Hint

Differentiate $F(x,\varphi(x))=0$ twice: $F_{xx}+2F_{xy}\varphi'+F_{yy}\varphi'^2+F_y\varphi''=0$. At a critical point ($\varphi'=0$) this simplifies.

## Solution

**Step 1: the critical-point condition.** $F_y=-x+2y$. Where $F_y\ne0$, $\varphi'=-\dfrac{F_x}{F_y}$, with $F_x=2x-y$. So $\varphi'=0\iff F_x=0\iff y=2x$.

**Step 2: the points.** Substitute $y=2x$ in $F=0$: $x^2-2x^2+4x^2=3x^2=3$, so $x=\pm1$ and $y=\pm2$: the points $(1,2)$ and $(-1,-2)$. At these points $F_y=-x+2y=-1+4=3$ and $=-3$ respectively: nonzero, so $\varphi$ exists near them and the points are indeed critical points of $\varphi$.

**Step 3: second derivative.** Differentiate $F(x,\varphi(x))=0$ twice. First $F_x+F_y\varphi'=0$; then
$$F_{xx}+2F_{xy}\varphi'+F_{yy}\varphi'^2+F_y\varphi''=0.$$
At a critical point $\varphi'=0$ this gives $\varphi''=-\dfrac{F_{xx}}{F_y}$. Here $F_{xx}=2$.
- At $(1,2)$: $F_y=3$, so $\varphi''=-\frac23<0$: a local **maximum** of $y$, equal to $2$.
- At $(-1,-2)$: $F_y=-3$, so $\varphi''=+\frac23>0$: a local **minimum** of $y$, equal to $-2$.

**Conclusion.** The curve is an ellipse (compact), so the global extrema of $y$ exist and are attained at critical points of $\varphi$ (or at points where $F_y=0$, which are the extreme points in $x$, where $y$ is not extreme as a function of $x$; here those points are $(2,1)$ and $(-2,-1)$, with $y=\pm1$, not extremal). Hence $y_{\max}=2$ and $y_{\min}=-2$.

**Check.** Complete the square: $x^2-xy+y^2=\left(x-\frac y2\right)^2+\frac34y^2=3$ gives $\frac34y^2\le3$, i.e. $|y|\le2$, with equality iff $x=\frac y2$: at $(1,2)$ and $(-1,-2)$ ✓.

**Common mistake.** Using $\varphi''=-F_{xx}/F_y$ at a point where $\varphi'\ne0$: the full formula with the terms $F_{xy}\varphi'$ and $F_{yy}\varphi'^2$ is needed in general.

**Remark.** This is the one-constraint case of Lagrange multipliers: maximise $y$ on $F=0$ means $\nabla y=(0,1)\parallel\nabla F=(2x-y,\,2y-x)$, i.e. $2x-y=0$: the same equation $F_x=0$.

---

---
title: Solving X + X³ = A for small matrices A
category: calculus/multivariable/diff-calculus/dc-implicit-function
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: matrix equation, implicit function theorem, Banach space, power series, uniqueness
order: 26
date: 2026-11-14
---

# Problem 26 — Solving $X+X^3=A$

## Statement

Let $\|\cdot\|$ be a submultiplicative norm on $M_n(\mathbb{R})$ and $F(X,A)=X+X^3-A$.

1. Show that there exist neighbourhoods $V$ of $0$ (for $A$) and $W$ of $0$ (for $X$) and a $C^\infty$ map $\varphi:V\to W$ with: for $(A,X)\in V\times W$, $X+X^3=A\iff X=\varphi(A)$.
2. Show that $\varphi(A)=A-A^3+3A^5-12A^7+\cdots$ as a convergent series for $\|A\|<\frac{2}{3\sqrt3}$, and that this is the unique small solution.
3. Deduce that $\varphi(A)$ commutes with $A$ and that if $\lambda$ is an eigenvalue of $A$ then $\psi(\lambda)$ is an eigenvalue of $\varphi(A)$, where $\psi$ is the scalar solution of $\psi+\psi^3=\lambda$.

## Hint

The differential of $X\mapsto X+X^3$ at $0$ is the identity: $H\mapsto H+(H\cdot0\cdot0+\ldots)=H$. For part 2 use the scalar series $\psi(t)=\sum_{k\ge0}(-1)^k\dfrac1{2k+1}\dbinom{3k}k\,t^{2k+1}$ of the solution of $\psi+\psi^3=t$.

## Solution

**Step 1: the implicit function theorem in a Banach space.** The space $E=M_n(\mathbb{R})$ is finite-dimensional ($\cong\mathbb{R}^{n^2}$), and $F:E\times E\to E$ is polynomial hence $C^\infty$. We have $F(0,0)=0$ and
$$D_XF(X,A)H=H+HX^2+XHX+X^2H,\qquad D_XF(0,0)=\mathrm{Id}_E,$$
invertible. By the implicit function theorem (the version for maps between finite-dimensional spaces, $m=n^2$ unknowns) there are neighbourhoods $V,W$ and a $C^\infty$ map $\varphi:V\to W$ as claimed.

**Step 2: the explicit solution.** The scalar equation $\psi+\psi^3=t$ near $t=0$ has the analytic solution
$$\psi(t)=t-t^3+3t^5-12t^7+55t^9-\cdots=\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{(-1)^k}{2k+1}\binom{3k}kt^{2k+1}$$
(Lagrange inversion: the coefficients $\frac1{2k+1}\binom{3k}k=1,1,3,12,55,\ldots$). Its radius of convergence is the distance to the nearest complex singularity, where $\frac{dt}{d\psi}=1+3\psi^2=0$, i.e. $\psi=\pm\frac i{\sqrt3}$, giving $t=\psi+\psi^3=\psi(1+\psi^2)=\pm\frac i{\sqrt3}\cdot\frac23=\pm\frac{2i}{3\sqrt3}$. So the radius is $\frac2{3\sqrt3}\approx0.385$ (the same number $\frac2{3\sqrt3}$ as in the cubic $x^3-x+t$ problem, up to the sign change $x\to ix$).

For $\|A\|<\frac2{3\sqrt3}$ the series $\psi(A):=\sum c_kA^{2k+1}$ converges absolutely in the Banach algebra $M_n(\mathbb{R})$. The identity $\psi(t)+\psi(t)^3=t$ holds as an identity of power series in one variable, and products of series in the *single* matrix $A$ behave exactly like products of scalar series (powers of $A$ commute), so $\psi(A)+\psi(A)^3=A$. Moreover $\psi$ is continuous with $\psi(0)=0$, so $\psi(A)\in W$ for $\|A\|$ small enough. By the uniqueness in Step 1, $\varphi(A)=\psi(A)$ for small $\|A\|$.

(Numerical check: for $A=\begin{pmatrix}0.1&0.2\\-0.1&0.15\end{pmatrix}$, the partial sum $A-A^3+3A^5-12A^7$ satisfies $X+X^3-A$ with an error of order $10^{-5}$, consistent with the neglected term $55A^9$.)

**Step 3: consequences.** Since $\varphi(A)$ is a limit of polynomials in $A$, it commutes with $A$, and the eigenvalues of $\varphi(A)=\psi(A)$ are $\psi(\lambda)$ for the eigenvalues $\lambda$ of $A$ (the spectral mapping theorem for convergent power series, e.g. by triangularising $A$ over $\mathbb{C}$).

**Common mistake.** Concluding that every solution of $X+X^3=A$ commutes with $A$. Uniqueness only holds among *small* $X$ (in $W$). For $A=0$, the equation $X(I+X^2)=0$ has, for $n=2$, the non-small solution $X=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ (a rotation by $90°$, with $X^2=-I$), which also gives $X+X^3=X-X=0$. So uniqueness is genuinely local.

**Remark.** The same method produces matrix versions of inverse functions: $\varphi$ is the "functional calculus" of the scalar inverse of $x\mapsto x+x^3$. The restriction to small $A$ is the matrix analogue of the restriction $|t|<\frac2{3\sqrt3}$, where the implicit function theorem for scalar $\psi$ gives an analytic solution; beyond this radius the scalar solution still exists on the real line (the cubic is monotone), but is no longer given by this series.

## Statement

Find and classify the critical points of $f(x,y)=x^2+y^2-2x-4y$.

## Hint

Set $\nabla f=0$; classify via the Hessian.

## Solution

**Gradient.** $\nabla f=(2x-2,\,2y-4)$, so the unique critical point is $(1,2)$.

**Hessian.** $H=\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&2\end{pmatrix}$: positive definite.

**Classification.** Global minimum, $f(1,2)=-5$.

**Conclusion.** Minimum at $(1,2)$ with value $-5$.

title: Two critical points and their nature
category: calculus/multivariable/constrained-optimisation

## Statement

Find and classify the critical points of $f(x,y)=4x+2y-x^2-y^2-2x^3$.

## Hint

Solve $f_x=4-2x-6x^2=0$, $f_y=2-2y=0$; then use the Hessian at each point.

## Solution

**Critical points.** $f_y=0\Rightarrow y=1$. $4-2x-6x^2=0\Rightarrow 3x^2+x-2=0\Rightarrow x=-1$ or $x=2/3$. Points: $A=(-1,1)$, $B=(2/3,1)$.

**Second derivatives.** $f_{xx}=-2-12x$, $f_{xy}=0$, $f_{yy}=-2$.

**At $A$.** $H=\begin{pmatrix}10&0\\0&-2\end{pmatrix}$, $\det=-20<0$: saddle.

**At $B$.** $H=\begin{pmatrix}-10&0\\0&-2\end{pmatrix}$, $\det=20>0$ and $f_{xx}<0$: local maximum. $f(B)=71/27$.

**Conclusion.** Saddle at $(-1,1)$, local maximum at $(2/3,1)$ with value $71/27$.

title: A unique critical point that is a saddle
category: calculus/multivariable/constrained-optimisation

## Statement

Find and classify the critical point of $f(x,y)=xy+x^3y^2$.

## Hint

$f_x=y(1+3x^2y)$, $f_y=x(1+2x^2y)$.

## Solution

**System.** $y(1+3x^2y)=0$ and $x(1+2x^2y)=0$. Only solution: $(0,0)$.

**Hessian at $(0,0)$.** $f_{xx}=6xy^2$, $f_{xy}=1+6x^2y$, $f_{yy}=2x^3$; $H(0,0)=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$.

**Determinant.** $\det H=-1<0$: saddle point.

**Conclusion.** The only critical point $(0,0)$ is a saddle.

@@@ constrained-higher-dim-saddle.md
---
title: Three-variable saddle point
category: calculus/multivariable/constrained-optimisation
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: three variables, saddle, Hessian
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find and classify the critical point of $f(x,y,z)=yz+zx-xy$.

## Hint

$\nabla f=(z-y,z-x,y+x)$; Hessian is constant.

## Solution

**Critical point.** Setting all partials to zero gives $x=y=z=0$.

**Hessian.**
$$H=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&1\\-1&0&1\\1&1&0\end{pmatrix}.$$

**Determinant.** $\det H=-2$, $\operatorname{tr}H=0$. Eigenvalues can't be all positive (product $-2$) or all negative (sum $0$).

**Conclusion.** The origin is a saddle point.

@@@ constrained-lagrange-circle.md

## Statement

Find the extrema of $f(x,y)=3x-y$ under the constraint $x^2+y^2=5$.

## Hint

$\nabla f=\lambda\nabla g$ with $g=x^2+y^2-5$.

## Solution

**Lagrange system.**
$$3=2\lambda x,\quad -1=2\lambda y,\quad x^2+y^2=5.$$

**Solve.** $x=3/(2\lambda)$, $y=-1/(2\lambda)$, so $y=-x/3$. Then $x^2+x^2/9=5\Rightarrow x^2=9/2\Rightarrow x=\pm3/\sqrt2$.

**Points.** $A=(3/\sqrt2,-1/\sqrt2)$, $B=(-3/\sqrt2,1/\sqrt2)$.

**Values.** $f(A)=5\sqrt2$ (maximum), $f(B)=-5\sqrt2$ (minimum).

**Conclusion.** Maximum $5\sqrt2$ at $A$, minimum $-5\sqrt2$ at $B$.

## Statement

Minimize $f(x,y)=x^2+y^2$ under $x+2y=5$.

## Hint

Substitute $y=(5-x)/2$ or use Lagrange.

## Solution

**Lagrange.** $2x=\lambda$, $2y=2\lambda\Rightarrow y=2x$; constraint gives $5x=5$, $x=1$, $y=2$.

**Value.** $f(1,2)=5$.

**Conclusion.** Minimum $5$ at $(1,2)$. The Lagrangian is convex so the critical point is a global minimum.

## Statement

Maximize $f(x,y)=(xy)^a$ for $x,y\ge0$, $a>0$, under $2x+3y=12$.

## Hint

Lagrange on the interior $x,y>0$; check the boundary separately.

## Solution

**Lagrange.** $a x^{a-1}y^a=2\lambda$, $a x^ay^{a-1}=3\lambda$. Ratio: $y/x=2/3$, i.e. $3y=2x$. Constraint: $2x+2x=12$, $x=3$, $y=2$.

**Boundary.** On $x=0$ or $y=0$, $f=0$. At $(3,2)$, $f=6^a>0$.

**Conclusion.** Global maximum $6^a$ at $(3,2)$.

@@@ constrained-lagrange-rectangle.md

title: Maximizing a volume with fixed perimeter
category: calculus/multivariable/constrained-optimisation
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Lagrange, geometric optimization, volume
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Maximize the volume $V=xyz$ of a rectangular box under the constraint $2(xy+yz+zx)=A$ (fixed surface area).

## Hint

By symmetry the optimum is the cube. Apply Lagrange to confirm.

## Solution

**Lagrange.** $\nabla(xyz)=\lambda\nabla(2(xy+yz+zx))$:
$$yz=2\lambda(y+z),\quad xz=2\lambda(x+z),\quad xy=2\lambda(x+y).$$

**Solve.** Dividing the first two gives $y/x=z/y$, so $x=y=z$ at the critical point.

**Conclusion.** Maximum volume when $x=y=z=\sqrt{A/6}$, giving $V=(A/6)^{3/2}$.

## Statement

Find the maximum distance from the origin to the ellipsoid $\frac{x^2}{a^2}+\frac{y^2}{b^2}+\frac{z^2}{c^2}=1$ with $0<a<b<c$.

## Hint

Maximize $x^2+y^2+z^2$ under the constraint; use Lagrange.

## Solution

**Points.** If $\lambda=a^2$, then $y=z=0$, $x=\pm a$: distance $a$. Similarly for $b,c$.

## Statement

Maximize $f(x,y)=x+y$ on the disk $x^2+y^2\le1$.

## Hint

Interior critical points or boundary via Lagrange.

## Solution

**Interior.** $\nabla f=(1,1)\neq(0,0)$: no interior critical point.

**Boundary.** Lagrange on $x^2+y^2=1$: $1=2\lambda x$, $1=2\lambda y\Rightarrow x=y$, so $x=y=\pm1/\sqrt2$.

**Values.** $f(1/\sqrt2,1/\sqrt2)=\sqrt2$ (max); $f(-1/\sqrt2,-1/\sqrt2)=-\sqrt2$ (min).

## Statement

Maximize $f(x,y,z)=xyz$ under $x+y+z=3$ and $x=y$.

## Hint

Substitute $y=x$ first, then Lagrange on two variables.

## Solution

**Substitute.** With $y=x$: $f(x,z)=x^2z$, constraint $2x+z=3$.

@@@ constrained-lagrange-maximum-compact.md
---
title: Global extrema on a compact set
category: calculus/multivariable/constrained-optimisation
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: compactness, global extrema, boundary
date: 2026-11-03

## Statement

Find the global extrema of $f(x,y)=x^2-y^2$ on the disk $x^2+y^2\le1$.

## Hint

Check the interior (Hessian indefinite), then the boundary (polar).

## Solution

**Interior.** $\nabla f=0\Rightarrow(0,0)$, saddle (Hessian indefinite).

**Boundary.** With $x=\cos t,y=\sin t$: $f=\cos^2t-\sin^2t=\cos 2t$.

**Values.** Range $[-1,1]$, with max $1$ at $(1,0)$ and min $-1$ at $(0,1)$.

**Conclusion.** Global max $1$, global min $-1$ on the boundary.

@@@ constrained-hessian-classify-3d.md
---
title: Classifying a 3D critical point
category: calculus/multivariable/constrained-optimisation
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Hessian, 3D, saddle
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Classify the critical point $(0,0,0)$ of $f(x,y,z)=x^2-y^2+z^2$.

## Hint

Hessian is diagonal with entries $(2,-2,2)$.

## Solution

**Hessian.** $\operatorname{diag}(2,-2,2)$.

**Eigenvalues.** $2,-2,2$: mixed signs.

**Conclusion.** Saddle point at the origin.

@@@ constrained-constrained-min-distance.md
---
title: Distance from point to plane
category: calculus/multivariable/constrained-optimisation
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Lagrange, distance, plane
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the shortest distance from $(1,1,1)$ to the plane $x+y+z=0$.

## Hint

Minimize $d^2=(x-1)^2+(y-1)^2+(z-1)^2$ under $x+y+z=0$.

## Solution

**Lagrange.** $2(x-1)=\lambda$, $2(y-1)=\lambda$, $2(z-1)=\lambda$, so $x=y=z$. Constraint: $3x=0$, so $x=y=z=0$.

**Distance.** $\sqrt{(1)^2+(1)^2+(1)^2}=\sqrt3$.

**Conclusion.** Distance $=\sqrt3$.

@@@ constrained-classify-boundary.md
---
title: Boundary classification
category: calculus/multivariable/constrained-optimisation
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: boundary, classification, circle
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find and classify the extrema of $f(x,y)=xy$ on the unit circle $x^2+y^2=1$.

## Hint

Parametrize the circle; $f=\frac12\sin 2\theta$.

## Solution

**Parametrization.** $x=\cos t$, $y=\sin t$, $f=\cos t\sin t=\frac12\sin 2t$.

**Extrema.** $f$ ranges in $[-1/2,1/2]$.

**Points.** Max $1/2$ at $t=\pi/4, 5\pi/4$; min $-1/2$ at $t=3\pi/4, 7\pi/4$.

**Conclusion.** Maximum $1/2$ at $(\pm1/\sqrt2,\pm1/\sqrt2)$ with matching signs; minimum $-1/2$ with opposite signs.

@@@ constrained-quadratic-form.md
---
title: Constrained quadratic form
category: calculus/multivariable/constrained-optimisation
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: quadratic form, Rayleigh quotient, eigenvalues
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Maximize $f(x,y)=3x^2+2xy+3y^2$ on the unit circle.

## Hint

The maximum is the largest eigenvalue of $\begin{pmatrix}3&1\\1&3\end{pmatrix}$.

## Solution

**Eigenvalues.** $(3-\lambda)^2-1=0\Rightarrow\lambda=2,4$.

**Maximum.** $\lambda_{\max}=4$, attained at the eigenvector $(1,1)/\sqrt2$.

**Conclusion.** Maximum $4$ at $(\pm1/\sqrt2,\pm1/\sqrt2)$ with equal signs.

@@@ constrained-cauchy-schwarz.md
---
title: Cauchy–Schwarz via optimization
category: calculus/multivariable/constrained-optimisation
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Cauchy-Schwarz, optimization, inequality
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Using Lagrange multipliers, prove that $x+y\le\sqrt{2(x^2+y^2)}$.

## Hint

Maximize $x+y$ under $x^2+y^2=1$; multiply by the radius.

## Solution

**Lagrange.** $1=2\lambda x$, $1=2\lambda y$, so $x=y$. On the unit circle, $x=y=1/\sqrt2$, giving maximum $\sqrt2$.

**Scaling.** For any $(x,y)\neq(0,0)$ with $r=\sqrt{x^2+y^2}$, $(x/r,y/r)$ is on the unit circle, so $x+y\le\sqrt2\,r$.

**Conclusion.** $x+y\le\sqrt{2(x^2+y^2)}$.

@@@ constrained-summary.md
---
title: Summary of constrained optimization
category: calculus/multivariable/constrained-optimisation
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: summary, constrained optimization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Summarize the key methods for constrained optimization.

## Hint

Review critical points, Hessian, Lagrange, boundary.

## Solution

**Unconstrained critical points.**
- Solve $\nabla f=0$.
- Classify via the Hessian: positive definite (min), negative definite (max), indefinite (saddle).

**Lagrange multipliers.** For $f$ under $g=0$: $\nabla f=\lambda\nabla g$, plus the constraint.

**Boundary.** Parameterize or restrict $f$ to the boundary surface; combine with interior results.

**Compact sets.** If the set is compact, global extrema exist; check interior, boundary, and corners.

**KKT for inequalities.** For $g\le0$: Lagrange with nonnegativity conditions.

**Examples.** Circles, lines, ellipsoids, quadratics, multi-constraint problems.

**Conclusion.** Constrained optimization reduces to finding critical points of the Lagrangian and evaluating on boundaries.

@@@ vector-line-integral-basic.md
---
title: Line integral of a scalar field
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: line integral, scalar field, parametrization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle\int_C(x+y)\,ds$ where $C$ is the segment from $(0,0)$ to $(1,1)$.

## Hint

Parametrize $\gamma(t)=(t,t)$, $t\in[0,1]$, and $ds=\|\gamma'(t)\|\,dt$.

## Solution

**Parametrization.** $\gamma(t)=(t,t)$, $\gamma'(t)=(1,1)$, $\|\gamma'(t)\|=\sqrt2$.

**Integral.**
$$\int_0^1(t+t)\sqrt2\,dt=2\sqrt2\int_0^1 t\,dt=\sqrt2.$$

**Conclusion.** $\int_C(x+y)\,ds=\sqrt2$.

@@@ vector-line-integral-work.md
---
title: Work done by a force field
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: work, line integral, vector field
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the work of $\vec F(x,y)=(y,x)$ along the segment from $(0,0)$ to $(1,2)$.

## Hint

Work $=\int_C\vec F\cdot d\vec r$. Parametrize $\gamma(t)=(t,2t)$.

## Solution

**Parametrization.** $\gamma(t)=(t,2t)$, $\gamma'(t)=(1,2)$, $t\in[0,1]$.

**Integrand.** $\vec F(\gamma(t))=(2t,t)$, $\vec F\cdot\gamma'=2t+2t=4t$.

**Integral.** $\int_0^1 4t\,dt=2$.

**Conclusion.** Work $=2$.

@@@ vector-line-integral-circle.md
---
title: Line integral along a circle
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: line integral, circle, parametrization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle\oint_C(-y\,dx+x\,dy)$ where $C$ is the unit circle oriented counterclockwise.

## Hint

$\gamma(t)=(\cos t,\sin t)$, $t\in[0,2\pi]$.

## Solution

**Parametrization.** $x=\cos t$, $y=\sin t$; $dx=-\sin t\,dt$, $dy=\cos t\,dt$.

**Integrand.** $-y\,dx+x\,dy=\sin^2t\,dt+\cos^2t\,dt=dt$.

**Integral.** $\oint_C = \int_0^{2\pi}dt=2\pi$.

**Conclusion.** $\oint_C(-y\,dx+x\,dy)=2\pi$ (twice the area enclosed).

@@@ vector-line-integral-piecewise.md

## Statement

Compute $\int_C\vec F\cdot d\vec r$ for $\vec F=(x^2,y^2)$ along the broken line from $(0,0)$ to $(1,0)$ to $(1,1)$.

## Hint

Split into two segments; sum the contributions.

## Solution

**Segment 1.** From $(0,0)$ to $(1,0)$: $\gamma_1(t)=(t,0)$, $t\in[0,1]$; $\vec F\cdot\gamma_1'=t^2$. Contribution $1/3$.

**Segment 2.** From $(1,0)$ to $(1,1)$: $\gamma_2(t)=(1,t)$; $\vec F\cdot\gamma_2'=t^2$. Contribution $1/3$.

**Total.** $2/3$.

**Conclusion.** $\int_C\vec F\cdot d\vec r=2/3$.

@@@ vector-line-conservative.md

title: Conservative vector field
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: conservative, gradient, path independence
date: 2026-11-03

## Statement

Show that $\vec F(x,y)=(2x,2y)$ is conservative and compute $\int_C\vec F\cdot d\vec r$ along any path from $(0,0)$ to $(1,2)$.

## Hint

Find a potential $\phi$ with $\nabla\phi=\vec F$.

## Solution

**Potential.** $\phi(x,y)=x^2+y^2$ satisfies $\nabla\phi=(2x,2y)=\vec F$.

**Path independence.** Integral $=\phi(1,2)-\phi(0,0)=1+4=5$.

**Conclusion.** The field is conservative; integral $=5$ regardless of path.

@@@ vector-line-work-check-conservative.md

title: Testing whether a field is conservative
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1

## Statement

Is $\vec F(x,y)=(x^2y,\,xy^2)$ conservative?

## Hint

For a field $(P,Q)$ in $\mathbb{R}^2$: check $P_y=Q_x$.

## Solution

**Compute.** $P=x^2y$, $Q=xy^2$. Then $P_y=x^2$, $Q_x=y^2$.

**Test.** $P_y\neq Q_x$ in general, so $\vec F$ is not conservative.

**Conclusion.** $\vec F$ is not conservative (curl nonzero).

@@@ vector-line-potential-function.md
---
title: Finding a potential function
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: potential, conservative, integration
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find a potential for $\vec F(x,y)=(e^x\cos y,\,-e^x\sin y)$.

## Hint

Integrate $P=e^x\cos y$ in $x$, then use the condition on $Q$.

## Solution

**Integrate $P$.** $\phi=\int e^x\cos y\,dx=e^x\cos y+g(y)$.

**Use $Q$.** $\phi_y=-e^x\sin y+g'(y)=-e^x\sin y$, so $g'(y)=0$.

**Potential.** $\phi(x,y)=e^x\cos y$.

**Conclusion.** $\vec F=\nabla\phi$ with $\phi=e^x\cos y$.

@@@ vector-line-fundamental-theorem.md
---
title: Fundamental theorem for line integrals
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: fundamental theorem, line integral, potential
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State the fundamental theorem for line integrals: if $\vec F=\nabla\phi$, then $\int_C\vec F\cdot d\vec r=\phi(B)-\phi(A)$.

## Hint

Apply the univariate chain rule along a parametrization.

## Solution

**Proof.** Let $\gamma(t)$, $t\in[a,b]$, parametrize $C$ from $A$ to $B$. Then
$$\int_C\vec F\cdot d\vec r=\int_a^b\nabla\phi(\gamma(t))\cdot\gamma'(t)\,dt=\int_a^b\frac{d}{dt}[\phi(\gamma(t))]\,dt=\phi(B)-\phi(A).$$

**Conclusion.** For conservative fields, the integral only depends on endpoints.

@@@ vector-line-closed.md
---
title: Closed paths in conservative fields
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: closed path, conservative, zero integral
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that if $\vec F$ is conservative and $C$ is closed, then $\oint_C\vec F\cdot d\vec r=0$.

## Hint

Use the fundamental theorem: $\phi(B)-\phi(A)$ with $A=B$.

## Solution

**Apply.** $\oint_C\vec F\cdot d\vec r=\phi(A)-\phi(A)=0$.

**Conclusion.** Conservative fields have zero circulation on closed loops.

@@@ vector-greens-theorem.md
---
title: Green's theorem
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Green theorem, curl, region
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Use Green's theorem to compute $\oint_C(-y\,dx+x\,dy)$ where $C$ is the unit circle oriented counterclockwise.

## Hint

$\oint_C(P\,dx+Q\,dy)=\iint_D(Q_x-P_y)\,dx\,dy$.

## Solution

**Identify.** $P=-y$, $Q=x$; $Q_x=1$, $P_y=-1$.

**Green.** $\oint=\iint_D(1-(-1))\,dx\,dy=2\,\text{Area}(D)=2\pi$.

**Conclusion.** $\oint_C(-y\,dx+x\,dy)=2\pi$.

@@@ vector-greens-theorem-general.md
---
title: Green's theorem with a general field
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Green, general field, region
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\oint_C(x^2-y^3)\,dx+(x^3+y^2)\,dy$ where $C$ is the circle $x^2+y^2=R^2$ (counterclockwise).

## Hint

$Q_x-P_y=3x^2+3y^2$.

## Solution

**Identify.** $P=x^2-y^3$, $Q=x^3+y^2$. Then $Q_x-P_y=3x^2+3y^2$.

**Green.** $\oint=3\iint_D(x^2+y^2)\,dx\,dy$.

**Polar.** $3\int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^Rr^3\,dr\,d\theta=3\cdot 2\pi\cdot\frac{R^4}{4}=\frac{3\pi R^4}{2}$.

**Conclusion.** $\oint=\frac{3\pi R^4}{2}$.

@@@ vector-greens-area.md

## Statement

Use Green's theorem to compute the area of the ellipse $\frac{x^2}{a^2}+\frac{y^2}{b^2}=1$.

## Hint

Area $=\frac12\oint_C(x\,dy-y\,dx)$.

## Solution

**Parametrization.** $\gamma(t)=(a\cos t,b\sin t)$, $t\in[0,2\pi]$.

**Compute $x\,dy-y\,dx$.** $x\,dy=ab\cos^2t\,dt$, $y\,dx=ab\sin^2t\,dt$.

**Integral.** $\frac12\int_0^{2\pi}ab\,dt=\pi ab$.

**Conclusion.** Area $=\pi ab$.

@@@ vector-divergence-definition.md

title: The divergence
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: divergence, vector field, computation
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\operatorname{div}\vec F$ for $\vec F=(x^2y,\,xy^2,\,z^3)$.

## Hint

$\operatorname{div}\vec F=\partial_xP+\partial_yQ+\partial_zR$.

## Solution

**Compute.** $\partial_x(x^2y)=2xy$, $\partial_y(xy^2)=2xy$, $\partial_z(z^3)=3z^2$.

**Divergence.** $\operatorname{div}\vec F=2xy+2xy+3z^2=4xy+3z^2$.

**Conclusion.** $\operatorname{div}\vec F=4xy+3z^2$.

@@@ vector-curl-definition.md
---
title: The curl
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: curl, vector field, rotation
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\nabla\times\vec F$ for $\vec F=(yz,\,xz,\,xy)$.

## Hint

$\nabla\times\vec F=(R_y-Q_z,\,P_z-R_x,\,Q_x-P_y)$.

## Solution

**Compute.**
- $R_y-Q_z=x-x=0$
- $P_z-R_x=y-y=0$
- $Q_x-P_y=z-z=0$

**Conclusion.** $\nabla\times\vec F=\vec0$: the field is irrotational. It is actually conservative with potential $\phi=xyz$.

@@@ vector-divergence-theorem.md
---
title: The divergence theorem
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: divergence theorem, flux, surface
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the flux of $\vec F=(x,y,z)$ through the sphere $S$ of radius $R$ centered at the origin, oriented outward.

## Hint

$\iint_S\vec F\cdot\vec n\,dS=\iiint_V\operatorname{div}\vec F\,dV$.

## Solution

**Divergence.** $\operatorname{div}\vec F=1+1+1=3$.

**Theorem.** Flux $=3\cdot\text{Vol}(V)=3\cdot\frac43\pi R^3=4\pi R^3$.

**Conclusion.** Flux $=4\pi R^3$.

@@@ vector-divergence-theorem-cube.md

## Statement

Compute the outward flux of $\vec F=(x^2,0,0)$ through the cube $[0,1]^3$.

## Hint

$\operatorname{div}\vec F=2x$; integrate over the cube.

## Solution

**Divergence.** $\operatorname{div}\vec F=2x$.

**Integral.** $\iiint_{[0,1]^3}2x\,dV=2\cdot\frac12=\frac12\cdot1=1$.

**Conclusion.** Flux $=1$.

## Statement

Compute $\oint_C\vec F\cdot d\vec r$ for $\vec F=(-y,x,0)$ where $C$ is the unit circle in the $z=0$ plane, counterclockwise.

**Normal.** $\vec n=(0,0,1)$.

@@@ vector-line-area-ellipse.md

title: Area of a region using line integral
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: line integral, area, polar
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Use $\frac12\oint(x\,dy-y\,dx)$ to compute the area of the region enclosed by $r=1+\cos\theta$ (cardioid).

## Hint

$x\,dy-y\,dx=r^2\,d\theta$ for polar curves.

## Solution

**Polar form.** $x=r\cos\theta$, $y=r\sin\theta$; direct computation shows $x\,dy-y\,dx=r^2\,d\theta$.

**Integral.** $\text{Area}=\frac12\int_0^{2\pi}(1+\cos\theta)^2\,d\theta$.

**Compute.** $(1+\cos\theta)^2=1+2\cos\theta+\cos^2\theta$; integral over $[0,2\pi]$: $2\pi+0+\pi=3\pi$.

**Result.** Area $=\frac12\cdot 3\pi=\frac{3\pi}{2}$.

**Conclusion.** Area of cardioid $=3\pi/2$.

## Statement

Compute $\oint_C\vec F\cdot d\vec r$ for $\vec F=(-y,x)$ around the annulus $1\le x^2+y^2\le4$, outer boundary counterclockwise, inner clockwise.

@@@ vector-greens-inverse.md
---
title: Recovering a field from its curl (2D)
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Green, curl, reconstruction
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Given $\vec F=(P,Q)$ with $Q_x-P_y=f$, find $P,Q$ via Green: express the circulation $\oint_C$ as $\iint_D f$.

## Hint

Green's theorem already relates the two.

## Solution

**Formula.** Green: $\oint_C(P\,dx+Q\,dy)=\iint_D(Q_x-P_y)\,dx\,dy=\iint_Df$.

**Application.** If $f=1$ and $D$ is the unit disk, circulation is $\pi$.

**Conclusion.** The circulation of any field with a given 2D curl on a region equals the integral of the curl.

@@@ vector-greens-symmetry.md
---
title: Green with symmetry
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Green, symmetry, shortcut
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\oint_C(x\,dy-y\,dx)$ around the square with vertices $(0,0),(1,0),(1,1),(0,1)$ counterclockwise.

## Hint

$Q_x-P_y=2$; multiply by area.

## Solution

**Green.** $Q_x-P_y=1+1=2$; area $=1$.

**Integral.** $2\cdot1=2$.

**Direct check.** Along edges: bottom $y=0$, $dy=0$, $x\,dy-y\,dx=0$; top $y=1$, $dy=0$, contribution $-1$ (going left); right edge $x=1$, $dx=0$, contribution $1$; left edge $x=0$, $dx=0$, contribution $0$. Total $1-1+0+... $ wait: edge 2 (right side going up) has $dx=0$, integrand $-y\cdot0=0$; top edge going left has $x\,dy=0$ and $-y\,dx$ with $y=1$, $dx$ negative, contribution $+1$; bottom $-y=0$, contribution $0$; left $dx=0$, contribution $0$. Total $=1$... hmm, let me redo. Actually the line integral $\oint(x\,dy-y\,dx)$: for the unit square, using $\frac12\oint(x\,dy-y\,dx)=$ area gives $\oint =2$. Green directly gives $2$.

**Conclusion.** $\oint=2$.

@@@ vector-stokes-torus.md
---
title: Stokes on a torus (statement)
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: Stokes, torus, closed surface
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the flux of any curl field through a closed surface is zero.

## Hint

$\iint_S(\nabla\times\vec F)\cdot\vec n\,dS=\iiint_V\operatorname{div}(\nabla\times\vec F)\,dV=0$.

## Solution

**Divergence of curl.** $\operatorname{div}(\nabla\times\vec F)=0$.

**Apply divergence theorem.** $\iint_S(\nabla\times\vec F)\cdot\vec n\,dS=\iiint_V0\,dV=0$.

**Conclusion.** Any curl field has zero flux through any closed surface.

@@@ vector-greens-reciprocity.md
---
title: Green's reciprocity
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: Green, reciprocity, symmetry
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State Green's reciprocity identity: for functions $u,v$ on a domain $D$ with boundary $\partial D$,
$$\iint_D(u\Delta v-v\Delta u)=\oint_{\partial D}\left(u\frac{\partial v}{\partial n}-v\frac{\partial u}{\partial n}\right)ds.$$

## Hint

Apply the divergence theorem to $\vec F=u\nabla v-v\nabla u$.

## Solution

**Divergence.** $\nabla\cdot(u\nabla v)=u\Delta v+\nabla u\cdot\nabla v$. Similarly for $v\nabla u$. Difference: $u\Delta v-v\Delta u$.

**Apply.** Divergence theorem gives the boundary identity.

**Conclusion.** Green's reciprocity identity follows from the divergence theorem.

@@@ vector-greens-application.md
---
title: An application of Green's theorem
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Green, application, area
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the area of the region bounded by $y=x^2$ and $y=x$ using the line integral $\frac12\oint(x\,dy-y\,dx)$.

## Hint

Parametrize each side and sum.

## Solution

**Intersection.** $x^2=x$ gives $x=0$ or $1$.

**Lower path.** $\gamma_1(t)=(t,t)$, $t\in[0,1]$. Contribution $\frac12\int_0^1(t\cdot1-t\cdot1)\,dt=0$.

**Upper path.** $\gamma_2(t)=(t,t^2)$ from $t=1$ to $0$ (reverse). Compute $\frac12\int_{1}^{0}(t\cdot2t-t^2\cdot1)\,dt=\frac12\int_1^0 t^2\,dt=\frac12\cdot(-\frac13)=-\frac16$; absolute value $\frac16$.

Wait, sign issue: total area should be positive. Let me just compute $|x^2-x|$ directly: area $=\int_0^1(x-x^2)\,dx=\frac12-\frac13=\frac16$.

**Conclusion.** Area $=1/6$.

@@@ vector-line-conservative-3d.md
---
title: Conservative fields in 3D
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: conservative, 3D, potential
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $\vec F=(yz,xz,xy)$ is conservative and find a potential.

## Hint

$\nabla\times\vec F=0$; find $\phi$ with $\nabla\phi=\vec F$.

## Solution

**Curl.** $\nabla\times\vec F=0$ (computed earlier), so $\vec F$ is conservative on $\mathbb{R}^3$.

**Potential.** Integrate $yz$ in $x$: $\phi=xyz+g(y,z)$. Then $\partial_y\phi=xz+g_y=xz$, so $g_y=0$; similarly $g_z=0$.

**Result.** $\phi=xyz$.

**Conclusion.** $\vec F=\nabla(xyz)$.

@@@ vector-divergence-gauss-plane.md

title: Flux through a flat surface
category: calculus/multivariable/vector-calculus
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: flux, plane surface, dot product
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the flux of $\vec F=(1,2,3)$ through the unit square in the plane $z=0$, oriented upward.

## Hint

Flux $=\vec F\cdot\vec n\cdot\text{area}$.

## Solution

**Normal.** $\vec n=(0,0,1)$.

**Flux.** $(1,2,3)\cdot(0,0,1)\cdot1=3$.

**Conclusion.** Flux $=3$.

@@@ multint-rectangle-double.md
---
title: Double integral over a rectangle
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: double integral, rectangle, iterated
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\iint_D 2y\sin x\,dx\,dy$ where $D=[0,\pi]\times[0,1]$.

## Hint

The bounds are independent: integrate one variable at a time.

## Solution

**Iterated integral.**
$$I=\int_0^{\pi}\int_0^1 2y\sin x\,dy\,dx.$$

**Inner.** $\int_0^1 2y\sin x\,dy=\sin x$.

**Outer.** $I=\int_0^{\pi}\sin x\,dx=[-\cos x]_0^{\pi}=2$.

**Conclusion.** $I=2$.

@@@ multint-under-parabola.md
---
title: Double integral under a parabola
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: double integral, type-I domain, parabola
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\iint_D x^2\,dx\,dy$ where $D=\{(x,y):x\le1,\ y\ge0,\ y^2\le x\}$.

## Hint

For $x\in[0,1]$, $y$ ranges from $0$ to $\sqrt x$.

## Solution

**Iterated.** $I=\int_0^1\int_0^{\sqrt x}x^2\,dy\,dx$.

**Inner.** $x^2\sqrt x$.

**Outer.** $I=\int_0^1 x^{5/2}\,dx=\frac{2}{7}$.

**Conclusion.** $I=2/7$.

@@@ multint-triangular-domain.md
---
title: Double integral over a triangular domain
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: triangle, double integral, bounds
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\iint_D \sin(x+y)\,dx\,dy$ where $D=\{x\ge0,\ y\ge0,\ x+y\le\pi\}$.

## Hint

For $x\in[0,\pi]$, $y$ ranges from $0$ to $\pi-x$.

## Solution

**Iterated.** $I=\int_0^{\pi}\int_0^{\pi-x}\sin(x+y)\,dy\,dx$.

**Inner.** $[-\cos(x+y)]_{y=0}^{y=\pi-x}=1+\cos x$.

**Outer.** $I=\int_0^{\pi}(1+\cos x)\,dx=[x+\sin x]_0^{\pi}=\pi$.

**Conclusion.** $I=\pi$.

@@@ multint-separable-rectangle.md
---
title: Separable integrand on a square
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: separability, product of integrals, square
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\iint_{[0,1]^2}\frac{x^2}{1+y^2}\,dx\,dy$.

## Hint

Factor as a product of one-dimensional integrals.

## Solution

**Factorisation.**
$$I=\left(\int_0^1 x^2\,dx\right)\left(\int_0^1\frac{dy}{1+y^2}\right)=\frac13\cdot\frac{\pi}{4}.$$

**Conclusion.** $I=\pi/12$.

@@@ multint-xy-symmetry.md
---
title: A symmetric triangle integral
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: symmetry, triangle, double integral
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\iint_D (x+y)\,dx\,dy$ where $D$ is the triangle with vertices $(0,0),(2,0),(0,2)$.

## Hint

The triangle is symmetric in $x$ and $y$: compute $\iint_D x$ only and multiply by $2$.

## Solution

**Setup.** $D=\{(x,y):0\le x\le2,\ 0\le y\le2-x\}$.

**Compute $\iint_D x$.**
$$\int_0^2\int_0^{2-x}x\,dy\,dx=\int_0^2 x(2-x)\,dx=\left[x^2-\frac{x^3}{3}\right]_0^2=4-\frac{8}{3}=\frac{4}{3}.$$

**Symmetry.** $\iint_D y=\frac43$, so $I=\frac83$.

**Conclusion.** $I=8/3$.

@@@ multint-polar-disk.md
---
title: Double integral over a disk in polar coordinates
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: polar coordinates, disk, Jacobian
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\iint_D\frac{dx\,dy}{\sqrt{1+x^2+y^2}}$ where $D=\{x^2+y^2\le1\}$.

## Hint

Polar: $x=r\cos\theta$, $y=r\sin\theta$, $dx\,dy=r\,dr\,d\theta$; $0\le r\le1$, $0\le\theta\le2\pi$.

## Solution

**Polar.**
$$I=\int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^1\frac{r}{\sqrt{1+r^2}}\,dr\,d\theta.$$

**Inner.** $[\sqrt{1+r^2}]_0^1=\sqrt2-1$.

**Outer.** $I=2\pi(\sqrt2-1)$.

**Conclusion.** $I=2\pi(\sqrt2-1)$.

@@@ multint-polar-annulus.md
---
title: Double integral over an annulus
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: polar coordinates, annulus, angular integral
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\iint_D\frac{dx\,dy}{x^2+y^2+xy}$ where $D=\{4\le x^2+y^2\le16\}$.

## Hint

Polar: $x^2+y^2+xy=r^2(1+\cos\theta\sin\theta)$.

## Solution

**Polar.** $I=\int_0^{2\pi}\int_2^4\frac{r\,dr\,d\theta}{r^2(1+\cos\theta\sin\theta)}=\int_2^4\frac{dr}{r}\cdot\int_0^{2\pi}\frac{d\theta}{1+\cos\theta\sin\theta}$.

**Radial.** $\int_2^4 dr/r=\ln2$.

**Angular integral.** Using $\cos\theta\sin\theta=\frac12\sin 2\theta$ and $t=\theta-\pi/2$, substitution $u=\tan t$:
$$J=\int_0^{2\pi}\frac{d\theta}{1+\frac12\sin2\theta}=2\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}\frac{dt}{1-\frac12\sin2t}=2\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\frac{du}{(u-\frac12)^2+\frac34}=\frac{4\pi}{\sqrt3}.$$

**Total.** $I=\frac{4\pi\ln2}{\sqrt3}$.

**Conclusion.** $I=\frac{4\pi}{\sqrt3}\ln2$.

@@@ multint-polar-cardioid.md
---
title: Double integral over a disk tangent at origin
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1

tags: polar coordinates, circle offset, integral
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\iint_D\frac{dx\,dy}{\sqrt{x^2+y^2}}$ where $D=\{y\ge0,\ x^2+y^2-2x\le0\}$.

## Hint

The disk $(x-1)^2+y^2\le1$ has polar equation $0\le r\le2\cos\theta$ with $\theta\in[0,\pi/2]$.

## Solution

**Polar.** $I=\int_0^{\pi/2}\int_0^{2\cos\theta}\frac{r}{r}\,dr\,d\theta=\int_0^{\pi/2}2\cos\theta\,d\theta=2$.

**Conclusion.** $I=2$.

@@@ multint-change-of-variables-linear.md
---
title: Linear change of variables
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: change of variables, Jacobian, triangle
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $I=\iint_D(x-y)\,dx\,dy$ where $D$ is bounded by $x=0$, $y=x+2$, $y=-x$, using the change of variables $u=x+y$, $v=x-y$.

## Hint

Jacobian $|J|=1/2$; transform the bounds.

## Solution

**Transformed region.** $D$: $0\le u\le 2$, $-2\le v\le -u$.

**Jacobian.** $x=\frac{u+v}{2}$, $y=\frac{u-v}{2}$, so $|J|=\frac12$.

**Compute.**
$$I=\frac12\int_0^2\int_{-2}^{-u}v\,dv\,du=\frac12\int_0^2\left(\frac{u^2}{2}-2\right)du=\frac12\left(\frac43-4\right)=-\frac43.$$

**Conclusion.** $I=-4/3$.

@@@ multint-change-of-variables-uv.md
---
title: Change of variables to simplify the integrand
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: change of variables, Jacobian, triangular
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle J=\iiint_D\frac{z^3}{(y+z)(x+y+z)}\,dx\,dy\,dz$ where $D=\{x,y,z\ge0,\ x+y+z\le1\}$ using $u=x+y+z$, $v=y+z$, $w=z$.

## Hint

$\det J_h=1$; the region becomes $0\le w\le v\le u\le1$.

## Solution

**Transformation.** $x=u-v$, $y=v-w$, $z=w$; $|J_h|=1$.

**Domain.** $\Delta=\{0\le w\le v\le u\le1\}$.

**Integrand.** $\frac{w^3}{uv}$.

**Compute.**
$$J=\int_0^1\int_0^u\int_0^v\frac{w^3}{uv}\,dw\,dv\,du=\int_0^1\int_0^u\frac{v^3}{4u}\,dv\,du=\int_0^1\frac{u^3}{16}\,du=\frac1{64}.$$

**Conclusion.** $J=1/64$.

@@@ multint-triple-simplex.md
---
title: Triple integral over a simplex
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: triple integral, simplex, iterated
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\iiint_D x\,dx\,dy\,dz$ where $D=\{x,y,z>0,\ x+y+z<1\}$.

## Hint

Integrate in the order $z,y,x$; bounds $0<z<1-x-y$.

## Solution

**Iterated.**
$$I=\int_0^1\int_0^{1-x}\int_0^{1-x-y}x\,dz\,dy\,dx=\int_0^1\int_0^{1-x}x(1-x-y)\,dy\,dx.$$

**Inner.** $x\frac{(1-x)^2}{2}$.

**Outer.** $I=\frac12\int_0^1 x(1-x)^2\,dx=\frac12\left(\frac12-\frac23+\frac14\right)=\frac1{24}$.

**Conclusion.** $I=1/24$.

@@@ multint-triple-cube-density.md

title: Mass of a cube with variable density
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: triple integral, density, cube
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the mass of $D=[0,1]^3$ with density $\mu(x,y,z)=x^2y+xz^2$.

## Hint

Integrate the density over the cube by separable terms.

## Solution

**Setup.**
$$M=\int_0^1\int_0^1\int_0^1(x^2y+xz^2)\,dz\,dy\,dx.$$

**Inner in $z$.** $x^2y+\frac x3$.

**In $y$.** $\frac{x^2}{2}+\frac x3$.

**In $x$.** $M=\frac16+\frac16=\frac13$.

**Conclusion.** $M=1/3$.

@@@ multint-centre-of-gravity.md
---
title: Centre of gravity of a planar region
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: centroid, parabola, uniform density
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the centroid of the region between $y=x$ and $y=6x-x^2$.

## Hint

Intersection points: $x=0$ and $x=5$. Use $\bar x=\frac{1}{A}\iint_D x$ and $\bar y=\frac{1}{A}\iint_D y$.

## Solution

**Area.**
$$A=\int_0^5(6x-x^2-x)\,dx=\int_0^5(5x-x^2)\,dx=\frac{125}{6}.$$

**$\bar x$.**
$$\iint_D x\,dx\,dy=\int_0^5 x(5x-x^2)\,dx=\frac{625}{12},$$
so $\bar x=\frac{625/12}{125/6}=\frac52$.

**$\bar y$.**
$$\iint_D y=\frac12\int_0^5\left[(6x-x^2)^2-x^2\right]dx=\frac{625}{3},$$
so $\bar y=\frac{625/3}{125/6}=10$... wait, careful: $\bar y=625/(3\cdot125/6)=10$? Check numeric: $625\cdot 6/(3\cdot125)=10$. But the geometric answer is $5$. Recompute carefully: $\iint_D y=\frac12\int_0^5(35x^2-12x^3+x^4)\,dx=\frac12\left(\frac{35\cdot125}{3}-3\cdot625+\frac{3125}{5}\right)=\frac12\left(\frac{4375}{3}-1875+625\right)$. Compute: $\frac{4375}{3}-1250=\frac{4375-3750}{3}=\frac{625}{3}$. So $\iint_D y=\frac{625}{6}$, and $\bar y=\frac{625/6}{125/6}=5$.

**Conclusion.** Centroid at $(5/2,5)$.

@@@ multint-fubini-trick.md
---
title: The Fubini trick
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Fubini, double integral, iterated
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\int_0^1\frac{\ln(1+x)}{1+x^2}\,dx$ using Fubini.

## Hint

Write $\ln(1+x)=\int_0^1\frac{x\,dy}{1+xy}$ and apply Fubini, then use symmetry in $x,y$.

## Solution

**Write as double integral.**
$$I=\int_0^1\int_0^1\frac{x}{(1+x^2)(1+xy)}\,dy\,dx.$$

**Symmetry.** Reversing $x\leftrightarrow y$:
$$2I=\int_0^1\int_0^1\frac{x+y}{(1+x^2)(1+y^2)}\,dx\,dy.$$

**Separate.**
$$2I=\left(\int_0^1\frac{x}{1+x^2}\,dx\right)\left(\int_0^1\frac{1}{1+y^2}\,dy\right)+\left(\int_0^1\frac{1}{1+x^2}\,dx\right)\left(\int_0^1\frac{y}{1+y^2}\,dy\right).$$
Both equal $\frac12\ln2\cdot\frac{\pi}{4}=\frac{\pi\ln2}{8}$. So $2I=\frac{\pi\ln2}{4}$, $I=\frac{\pi\ln2}{8}$.

**Conclusion.** $I=\frac{\pi\ln2}{8}$.

@@@ multint-polar-annular-region.md
---
title: Polar region between two circles
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: polar, circle, region between circles
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\iint_D\sqrt{x^2+y^2}\,dx\,dy$ where $D=\{x^2+y^2\ge2y,\ x^2+y^2\le1\}$.

## Hint

In polar: $x^2+y^2\le1$ gives $r\le1$; $x^2+y^2\ge2y$ gives $r\ge2\sin\theta$. Determine the range of $\theta$.

## Solution

**Polar.** $\sin\theta\le1/2$ for the two-disk overlap; the region splits into $\theta\in[0,\pi/6]$, $[5\pi/6,\pi]$, $[\pi,2\pi]$.

**Compute each piece.**
- $I_1$ over $[\pi,2\pi]$, $0\le r\le1$: $I_1=\int_\pi^{2\pi}\int_0^1r^2\,dr\,d\theta=\frac{\pi}{3}$.
- $I_2$ over $[0,\pi/6]$, $2\sin\theta\le r\le1$: $I_2=\frac{\pi}{18}+\sqrt3-\frac{16}{9}$.
- $I_3$ over $[5\pi/6,\pi]$, by symmetry $I_3=I_2$.

**Sum.** $I=\frac{\pi}{3}+\frac{2\pi}{18}+2\sqrt3-\frac{32}{9}=\frac{4\pi-32}{9}+2\sqrt3$.

**Conclusion.** $I=\frac{4\pi-32}{9}+2\sqrt3$.

@@@ multint-triple-with-sin.md
---
title: Triple integral with sine
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: triple integral, sine, simplex
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle \iiint_V\frac{\sin x}{\pi-x-y}\,dx\,dy\,dz$ where $V=\{x,y,z\ge0,\ x+y+z\le\pi\}$.

## Hint

Integrate in $z$ first: the inner integral is $\sin x$.

## Solution

**Iterated.**
$$I=\int_0^\pi\int_0^{\pi-x}\int_0^{\pi-x-y}\frac{\sin x}{\pi-x-y}\,dz\,dy\,dx.$$

**Inner in $z$.** $z$ from $0$ to $\pi-x-y$: the inner integral equals $\sin x$.

**In $y$.** $\int_0^{\pi-x}\sin x\,dy=(\pi-x)\sin x$.

**In $x$.** $I=\int_0^\pi(\pi-x)\sin x\,dx=\pi\int_0^\pi\sin x-\int_0^\pi x\sin x=\pi\cdot2-\pi=\pi$.

**Conclusion.** $I=\pi$.

@@@ multint-triple-cube.md
---
title: Triple integral over a cube
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: cube, triple integral, iterated
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\iiint_{[0,1]^3}\frac{dx\,dy\,dz}{(x+y+z+1)^3}$.

## Hint

Integrate in $x$ first: $\int_0^1\frac{dx}{(x+c)^3}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{c^2}-\frac{1}{(c+1)^2}\right)$.

## Solution

**Inner in $x$.** $\frac12\left(\frac{1}{(y+z+1)^2}-\frac{1}{(y+z+2)^2}\right)$.

**In $y$.** $\frac12\left(\frac{1}{z+1}-\frac{2}{z+2}+\frac{1}{z+3}\right)$.

**In $z$.** $I=\frac12\left(\ln2-2\ln\frac32+\ln\frac43\right)=\frac{5\ln2-3\ln3}{2}$.

**Conclusion.** $I=\frac{5\ln2-3\ln3}{2}$.

@@@ multint-triple-simplex-p3.md
---
title: Triple integral over a simplex with cube
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: simplex, triple integral, iterated
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\iiint_D\frac{dx\,dy\,dz}{(x+y+z+1)^3}$ where $D=\{x,y,z\ge0,\ x+y+z\le1\}$.

## Hint

Order $z,y,x$: $0\le z\le1-x-y$.

## Solution

**Inner in $z$.**
$$\int_0^{1-x-y}\frac{dz}{(x+y+z+1)^3}=\frac{1}{2(x+y+1)^2}-\frac18.$$

**In $y$.** $\frac12\int_0^{1-x}\frac{dy}{(x+y+1)^2}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{x+1}-\frac12\right)$, minus $\frac18(1-x)$.

**In $x$.** 
$$I=\frac12\int_0^1\left(\frac{1}{x+1}-\frac12\right)dx-\frac18\cdot\frac12=\frac12\left(\ln2-\frac12\right)-\frac{1}{16}.$$

**Result.** $I=\frac12\ln2-\frac{5}{16}$.

**Conclusion.** $I=\frac12\ln2-\frac{5}{16}$.

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\iint_D\sqrt{x^2+y^2}\,dx\,dy$ where $D$ is the disk of radius $1$ centered at $(0,0)$ intersected with the half-plane $y\ge0$.

@@@ multint-spherical-volume.md
---
title: Volume of a sphere by triple integral
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: spherical coordinates, volume, sphere
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the volume of the ball $B_R=\{x^2+y^2+z^2\le R^2\}$ using spherical coordinates.

## Hint

$x=r\sin\varphi\cos\theta$, $y=r\sin\varphi\sin\theta$, $z=r\cos\varphi$; Jacobian $r^2\sin\varphi$.

## Solution

**Spherical.** $0\le r\le R$, $0\le\varphi\le\pi$, $0\le\theta\le2\pi$.

**Jacobian.** $r^2\sin\varphi$.

**Compute.**
$$V=\int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^\pi\int_0^R r^2\sin\varphi\,dr\,d\varphi\,d\theta=2\pi\cdot2\cdot\frac{R^3}{3}=\frac{4\pi R^3}{3}.$$

**Conclusion.** $V=\frac{4\pi R^3}{3}$.

@@@ multint-spherical-radial.md
---
title: Spherical coordinates with a radial integrand
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: spherical coordinates, radial, integration
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle I=\iiint_{B_R}e^{-\sqrt{x^2+y^2+z^2}}\,dx\,dy\,dz$ where $B_R$ is the ball of radius $R$.

## Hint

Spherical: integrand $e^{-r}$, Jacobian $r^2\sin\varphi$.

## Solution

**Setup.**
$$I=\int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^\pi\int_0^R e^{-r}r^2\sin\varphi\,dr\,d\varphi\,d\theta=4\pi\int_0^R r^2e^{-r}\,dr.$$

**Compute.** $\int_0^R r^2e^{-r}\,dr=2-(R^2+2R+2)e^{-R}$ (two integrations by parts).

**Result.** $I=4\pi[2-(R^2+2R+2)e^{-R}]$.

**Conclusion.** $I=4\pi\left(2-(R^2+2R+2)e^{-R}\right)$.

@@@ multint-cylindrical-volume.md
---
title: Volume using cylindrical coordinates
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: cylindrical coordinates, volume, cone
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the volume of the solid bounded by $z=0$, $z=h$ and $x^2+y^2\le R^2$.

## Hint

Cylindrical: $x=r\cos\theta$, $y=r\sin\theta$, $z=z$; Jacobian $r$.

## Solution

**Cylindrical.** $0\le r\le R$, $0\le\theta\le2\pi$, $0\le z\le h$.

**Volume.** $V=\int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^R\int_0^h r\,dz\,dr\,d\theta=2\pi\cdot\frac{R^2}{2}\cdot h=\pi R^2h$.

**Conclusion.** $V=\pi R^2h$.

@@@ multint-cartesian-vs-polar.md
---
title: Cartesian vs polar computation
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: polar vs cartesian, comparison, disk
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compare the difficulty of computing $\iint_{x^2+y^2\le1}(x^2+y^2)\,dx\,dy$ in Cartesian and polar coordinates.

## Hint

Cartesian: use $y=\pm\sqrt{1-x^2}$. Polar: integrand $r^2$, Jacobian $r$.

## Solution

**Cartesian.** $I=\int_{-1}^1\int_{-\sqrt{1-x^2}}^{\sqrt{1-x^2}}(x^2+y^2)\,dy\,dx$: requires trigonometric substitutions.

**Polar.** $I=\int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^1 r^3\,dr\,d\theta=2\pi\cdot\frac14=\frac{\pi}{2}$.

**Conclusion.** Polar coordinates give an immediate result; Cartesian is much more involved.

@@@ multint-boundary-terms.md
---
title: Boundary effects
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: boundary, integration bounds, evaluation
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute $\displaystyle\iint_D xy\,dx\,dy$ where $D=\{(x,y):0\le x\le y\le1\}$.

## Hint

Triangle: $0\le x\le y\le1$, so for $y\in[0,1]$, $x$ runs from $0$ to $y$; or for $x\in[0,1]$, $y$ runs from $x$ to $1$.

## Solution

**Iterated.** $\int_0^1\int_x^1 xy\,dy\,dx=\int_0^1 x\frac{1-x^2}{2}\,dx=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac12-\frac14\right)=\frac18$.

**Conclusion.** $I=1/8$.

@@@ multint-exponential-gaussian.md
---
title: Gaussian integral over $\mathbb{R}^2$
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: Gaussian, polar, improper integral
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}^2}e^{-(x^2+y^2)}\,dx\,dy=\pi$.

## Hint

Polar coordinates: $r^2=x^2+y^2$, Jacobian $r$.

## Solution

**Polar.** $\int_0^{2\pi}\int_0^{+\infty}e^{-r^2}r\,dr\,d\theta=2\pi\cdot\frac12=\pi$.

**Conclusion.** $\iint_{\mathbb{R}^2}e^{-(x^2+y^2)}\,dx\,dy=\pi$.

**Corollary.** $\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}e^{-x^2}\,dx=\sqrt\pi$ (Fubini argument).

@@@ multint-jacobian-transform.md
---
title: Jacobian of a nonlinear transformation
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: Jacobian, nonlinear, change of variables
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that under the change $(u,v)=(x^2-y^2,2xy)$, the Jacobian is $4(x^2+y^2)$.

## Hint

Compute the $2\times 2$ determinant of partials.

## Solution

**Jacobian.** $\det\begin{pmatrix}2x&-2y\\2y&2x\end{pmatrix}=4x^2+4y^2$.

**Conclusion.** $|J|=4(x^2+y^2)$.

@@@ multint-summary.md
---
title: Summary of multiple integrals
category: calculus/multivariable/multiple-integrals
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: summary, multiple integrals
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Summarize key tools for multiple integrals.

## Hint

Review Fubini, change of variables, polar, spherical, cylindrical.

## Solution

**Fubini.** Iterated integration on product domains.

**Change of variables.** $dx\,dy\to|J|\,du\,dv$; $|J|$ is the Jacobian determinant.

**Polar.** $x=r\cos\theta$, $y=r\sin\theta$, $dx\,dy=r\,dr\,d\theta$.

**Cylindrical.** $x=r\cos\theta$, $y=r\sin\theta$, $z=z$, $dV=r\,dr\,d\theta\,dz$.

**Spherical.** $x=r\sin\varphi\cos\theta$, $y=r\sin\varphi\sin\theta$, $z=r\cos\varphi$, $dV=r^2\sin\varphi\,dr\,d\varphi\,d\theta$.

**Applications.** Volumes, centroids, masses with variable density, Gaussian integrals.

**Conclusion.** Multiple integrals reduce to iterated integrals via Fubini, and change of variables exploits symmetries to simplify computations.

@@@ partial-derivatives-first-computation.md
---
title: First partial derivatives
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: partial derivatives, definition, computation
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f(x,y)=x^2y+3xy^2-2x+5$. Compute $\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}$ and $\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}$.

## Hint

Differentiate with respect to one variable, treating the other as a constant.

## Solution

**With respect to $x$.**
$$\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}=2xy+3y^2-2.$$

**With respect to $y$.**
$$\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}=x^2+6xy.$$

**Conclusion.** The two partial derivatives are as above.

@@@ partial-derivatives-evaluation.md
---
title: Evaluating partial derivatives at a point
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: partial derivatives, evaluation, point
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f(x,y)=e^{xy}+\ln(1+x^2)$. Compute $\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(0,1)$ and $\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}(0,1)$.

## Hint

Compute the partial derivatives, then substitute $(x,y)=(0,1)$.

## Solution

**Partial derivatives.**
$$\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}=ye^{xy}+\frac{2x}{1+x^2},\qquad \frac{\partial f}{\partial y}=xe^{xy}.$$

**At $(0,1)$.** $\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(0,1)=1\cdot1+0=1$, $\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}(0,1)=0$.

**Conclusion.** The values are $1$ and $0$.

@@@ partial-derivatives-gradient.md

title: The gradient
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: lycee

date: 2026-11-03

## Statement

Let $f(x,y,z)=x^2+y^2+z^2$. Compute the gradient $\nabla f$ and evaluate it at $(1,2,2)$.

## Hint

$\nabla f=(\partial_x f,\partial_y f,\partial_z f)$.

## Solution

**Gradient.** $\nabla f(x,y,z)=(2x,2y,2z)$.

**At $(1,2,2)$.** $\nabla f(1,2,2)=(2,4,4)$.

**Interpretation.** The gradient points in the direction of steepest increase of $f$, with magnitude $\|\nabla f\|=\sqrt{4+16+16}=6$.

**Conclusion.** $\nabla f(1,2,2)=(2,4,4)$.

@@@ partial-derivatives-directional.md

## Statement

Let $f(x,y)=x^2y$ and $\vec u=(1,1)/\sqrt2$. Compute the directional derivative $D_{\vec u}f(1,2)$.

## Hint

$D_{\vec u}f=\nabla f\cdot\vec u$.

## Solution

**Gradient.** $\nabla f=(2xy,x^2)$, so $\nabla f(1,2)=(4,1)$.

**Dot product.** $D_{\vec u}f(1,2)=(4,1)\cdot\frac{(1,1)}{\sqrt2}=\frac{5}{\sqrt2}$.

**Conclusion.** $D_{\vec u}f(1,2)=5/\sqrt2$.

@@@ partial-derivatives-chain-general.md
---
title: General chain rule
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: chain rule, composition, multivariable
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f(u,v)=u^2v$ and $u(x,y)=x+y$, $v(x,y)=xy$. Compute $\frac{\partial}{\partial x}f(u,v)$ and $\frac{\partial}{\partial y}f(u,v)$.

## Hint

Apply the chain rule:
$$\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}=f_u u_x+f_v v_x.$$

## Solution

**Partial derivatives of $f$.** $f_u=2uv$, $f_v=u^2$.

**Chain rule for $\partial_x$.**
$$\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}=2uv\cdot1+u^2\cdot y=2(x+y)(xy)+y(x+y)^2=(x+y)(2xy+y(x+y)).$$

**Chain rule for $\partial_y$.**
$$\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}=2uv\cdot1+u^2\cdot x=2(x+y)(xy)+x(x+y)^2=(x+y)(2xy+x(x+y)).$$

**Conclusion.** Both partial derivatives are polynomials in $x,y$.

@@@ partial-derivatives-higher-order.md
---
title: Second-order partial derivatives
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: higher order, Schwarz, second derivatives
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f(x,y)=x^3y^2+xy$. Compute all four second-order partial derivatives and check Schwarz's theorem.

## Hint

Differentiate $f_x$ and $f_y$ once more; compare $f_{xy}$ and $f_{yx}$.

## Solution

**First derivatives.** $f_x=3x^2y^2+y$, $f_y=2x^3y+x$.

**Second derivatives.** $f_{xx}=6xy^2$, $f_{yy}=2x^3$, $f_{xy}=6x^2y+1$, $f_{yx}=6x^2y+1$.

**Conclusion.** $f_{xy}=f_{yx}$: Schwarz's theorem holds (the mixed partials are equal because $f$ is $C^2$).

@@@ partial-derivatives-schwarz.md
---
title: Schwarz's theorem and a counterexample
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Schwarz theorem, mixed partials, symmetry
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State Schwarz's theorem on mixed partial derivatives. Then consider the function
$$f(x,y)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{xy(x^2-y^2)}{x^2+y^2}&\text{if }(x,y)\neq(0,0),\\[2mm]0&\text{if }(x,y)=(0,0).\end{cases}$$
Compute $f_{xy}(0,0)$ and $f_{yx}(0,0)$ and verify that they differ. Explain why this does not contradict Schwarz's theorem.

## Hint

Compute the first partial derivatives at $(0,0)$ via the definition, then the second mixed partials via the definition at the origin. Schwarz's theorem requires $C^2$ regularity near the point.

## Solution

**Schwarz's theorem.** If $f$ has continuous second partial derivatives in a neighbourhood of a point $(a,b)$, then $f_{xy}(a,b)=f_{yx}(a,b)$.

**First partials at the origin.** For $x\neq0$,
$$\frac{f(x,0)-f(0,0)}{x}=\frac{0}{x}=0,$$
so $f_x(0,0)=0$. Similarly $f_y(0,0)=0$.

**Mixed partials at the origin.**
$$f_{xy}(0,0)=\lim_{y\to0}\frac{f_x(0,y)-f_x(0,0)}{y},\qquad f_{yx}(0,0)=\lim_{x\to0}\frac{f_y(x,0)-f_y(0,0)}{x}.$$
A direct computation gives $f_{xy}(0,0)=-1$ and $f_{yx}(0,0)=1$.

**Why no contradiction.** The second partial derivatives are not continuous at $(0,0)$: along paths approaching the origin, $f_{xy}$ and $f_{yx}$ do not admit a limit equal to $-1$ and $1$ respectively. Schwarz's hypothesis ($C^2$ regularity near the point) fails, so the conclusion need not hold.

**Conclusion.** Schwarz's theorem is compatible with the existence of such counterexamples: only $C^2$ functions are guaranteed to have symmetric mixed partials.
@@@ partial-derivatives-tangent-plane.md
---
title: Tangent plane to a graph
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: tangent plane, differentiability, graph
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the tangent plane to the surface $z=x^2+y^2$ at the point $(1,2,5)$.

## Hint

The tangent plane is $z=f(a,b)+f_x(a,b)(x-a)+f_y(a,b)(y-b)$.

## Solution

**Partial derivatives.** $f_x=2x$, $f_y=2y$; at $(1,2)$: $f_x=2$, $f_y=4$.

**Tangent plane.** $z=5+2(x-1)+4(y-2)=2x+4y-5$.

**Conclusion.** The tangent plane is $z=2x+4y-5$.

@@@ partial-derivatives-differentiability.md
---
title: Differentiability of a piecewise function
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: differentiability, continuity, piecewise
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let
$$f(x,y)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{xy}{\sqrt{x^2+y^2}}&\text{if }(x,y)\neq(0,0),\\0&\text{if }(x,y)=(0,0).\end{cases}$$
Show that $f$ is continuous at $(0,0)$ and compute its partial derivatives there.

## Hint

Bound $|xy|\le(x^2+y^2)/2$ and use the definition of partials.

## Solution

**Continuity.** $|f(x,y)|\le\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{x^2+y^2}\to0$; so $f$ is continuous at the origin.

**Partial derivatives.** $f_x(0,0)=\lim_{h\to0}\frac{f(h,0)-0}{h}=0$; similarly $f_y(0,0)=0$.

**Conclusion.** $f$ is continuous with $f_x(0,0)=f_y(0,0)=0$. (However, $f$ is not differentiable at $(0,0)$: check along $y=x$.)

@@@ partial-derivatives-differential.md
---
title: The differential
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: differential, linearization, approximation
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f(x,y)=x^3+y^3$. Compute the differential at $(1,2)$ and use it to approximate $f(1.01,1.99)$.

## Hint

$df=f_x(a,b)\,dx+f_y(a,b)\,dy$ with $dx=0.01$, $dy=-0.01$.

## Solution

**Partial derivatives at $(1,2)$.** $f_x=3x^2=3$, $f_y=3y^2=12$.

**Differential.** $df=3\,dx+12\,dy$.

**Approximation.** $f(1,2)=9$, so $f(1.01,1.99)\approx9+3(0.01)+12(-0.01)=9-0.09=8.91$.

**Exact value.** $1.01^3+1.99^3\approx1.0303+7.8806=8.9109$, very close.

**Conclusion.** Linear approximation gives $\approx8.91$.

@@@ partial-derivatives-level-curves.md
---
title: Level curves and gradients
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: level curves, gradient, orthogonality
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For $f(x,y)=x^2+y^2$, describe the level curves and show that the gradient is orthogonal to them.

## Hint

Level curve $f=c$ is a circle; parameterize and differentiate.

## Solution

**Level curves.** $f=c$ is the circle $x^2+y^2=c$ of radius $\sqrt c$ (for $c>0$).

**Parameterization.** $\gamma(t)=(\sqrt c\cos t,\sqrt c\sin t)$; $\gamma'(t)=\sqrt c(-\sin t,\cos t)$ is tangent.

**Gradient.** $\nabla f=2(x,y)=2\gamma(t)$, radial and orthogonal to $\gamma'(t)$:
$$(2\sqrt c\cos t,2\sqrt c\sin t)\cdot(-\sqrt c\sin t,\sqrt c\cos t)=0.$$

**Conclusion.** $\nabla f$ is orthogonal to the level curves; it points in the direction of fastest increase of $f$.

@@@ partial-derivatives-implicit.md
---
title: Implicit differentiation
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: implicit differentiation, Implicit Function Theorem
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

The curve $x^2+y^2=1$ defines $y$ implicitly as a function of $x$ near $(1/2,\sqrt3/2)$. Compute $y'(x)$ by implicit differentiation.

## Hint

Differentiate $x^2+y(x)^2=1$ with respect to $x$.

## Solution

**Differentiation.** $2x+2y(x)y'(x)=0$.

**Solve.** $y'(x)=-x/y(x)$.

**At $x=1/2$.** $y=\sqrt3/2$, so $y'(1/2)=-\frac{1/2}{\sqrt3/2}=-1/\sqrt3$.

**Conclusion.** $y'(1/2)=-1/\sqrt3$, consistent with the geometric interpretation of a tangent to the circle.

@@@ partial-derivatives-inverse-function.md
---
title: Inverse function theorem (illustration)
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: inverse function theorem, Jacobian, local invertibility
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $F(x,y)=(e^x\cos y,e^x\sin y)$. Compute the Jacobian matrix at $(0,0)$ and check the inverse function theorem.

## Hint

Jacobian columns: derivatives with respect to $x$ and $y$.

## Solution

**Partial derivatives.** $\partial_x F=(e^x\cos y,e^x\sin y)$, $\partial_y F=(-e^x\sin y,e^x\cos y)$.

**Jacobian at $(0,0)$.** $J_F(0,0)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$.

**Conclusion.** $\det J=1\neq0$, so the inverse function theorem applies: $F$ is a local diffeomorphism near $(0,0)$.

@@@ partial-derivatives-jacobian-composition.md
---
title: Jacobian of a composition
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Jacobian, composition, chain rule
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State the chain rule in matrix form: $J_{F\circ G}(x)=J_F(G(x))\cdot J_G(x)$.

## Hint

This is the multivariable analogue of $(f\circ g)'=f'(g)\cdot g'$.

## Solution

**Statement.** If $G:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}^m$ and $F:\mathbb{R}^m\to\mathbb{R}^p$ are differentiable, then
$$J_{F\circ G}(x)=J_F(G(x))\cdot J_G(x).$$

**Application.** Computing derivatives of composite functions via matrix multiplication.

**Conclusion.** The Jacobian of a composition is the product of Jacobians.

@@@ partial-derivatives-clairaut.md
---
title: Clairaut's theorem
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Clairaut, Schwarz, mixed partials
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

State Clairaut's theorem (Schwarz) and give a sufficient condition for $f_{xy}=f_{yx}$.

## Hint

Continuity of second partials is sufficient.

## Solution

**Theorem.** If $f$ has continuous second partial derivatives in a neighbourhood of a point, then $f_{xy}=f_{yx}$ at that point.

**Sufficient condition.** $f$ is $C^2$.

**Conclusion.** Mixed partials commute under $C^2$ regularity.

@@@ partial-derivatives-optimisation-first.md
---
title: Finding critical points
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: critical points, stationary, gradient
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the critical points of $f(x,y)=x^2+y^2-2x-4y$.

## Hint

Set both partial derivatives to zero.

## Solution

**Gradient.** $\nabla f=(2x-2,2y-4)$.

**Critical point.** $2x-2=0\Rightarrow x=1$; $2y-4=0\Rightarrow y=2$. So $(1,2)$.

**Nature.** Rewrite $f=(x-1)^2+(y-2)^2-5$: minimum at $(1,2)$.

**Conclusion.** The unique critical point is $(1,2)$, a global minimum.

@@@ partial-derivatives-hessian.md
---
title: The Hessian matrix
category: calculus/multivariable/constrained-optimisation
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Hessian, second derivatives, quadratic form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Compute the Hessian of $f(x,y)=x^2+4xy+3y^2$ at $(0,0)$ and classify the critical point.

## Hint

The Hessian is the matrix of second partials; classify via its eigenvalues.

## Solution

**Partial derivatives.** $f_x=2x+4y$, $f_y=4x+6y$; $f_{xx}=2$, $f_{yy}=6$, $f_{xy}=4$.

**Hessian.** $H=\begin{pmatrix}2&4\\4&6\end{pmatrix}$.

**Eigenvalues.** $\det(H-\lambda I)=(2-\lambda)(6-\lambda)-16=\lambda^2-8\lambda-4$; roots $\lambda=4\pm2\sqrt5$. One positive, one negative.

**Conclusion.** The origin is a saddle point (Hessian is indefinite).

@@@ partial-derivatives-taylor-2.md
---
title: Taylor expansion of order 2
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Taylor expansion, multivariable, approximation
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Give the Taylor expansion of $f(x,y)=e^{x+y}$ at $(0,0)$ up to order $2$.

## Hint

$f(0)=1$, $\nabla f (0,0)=(1,1)$, $H(0,0)=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}$.

## Solution

**Expression.**
$$f(h,k)=f(0,0)+ \nabla f(0,0) \cdot (h,k)+\frac{1}{2}(h,k) \cdot H(0,0) \cdot (h,k) +o((h^2+k^2)).$$

**Simplified.** $f(h,k)=1+h+k+\frac{h^2}{2}+hk+\frac{k^2}{2}+o(h^2+k^2)$.

**Conclusion.** The expansion matches $e^h\cdot e^k=(1+h+h^2/2+\cdots)(1+k+k^2/2+\cdots)$ to order $2$.

@@@ partial-derivatives-not-differentiable.md
---
title: Partial derivatives exist but function is not differentiable
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: differentiability, counterexample, partial derivatives
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $f(x,y)=\dfrac{xy}{x^2+y^2}$ for $(x,y)\neq(0,0)$, $f(0,0)=0$, has partial derivatives at $(0,0)$ but is not continuous there.

## Hint

Compute partial derivatives along the axes; check continuity along $y=x$.

## Solution

**Partial derivatives.** $f_x(0,0)=\lim_{h\to0}\frac{f(h,0)-0}{h}=0$; $f_y(0,0)=0$.

**Continuity.** Along $y=x$: $f(x,x)=\frac{x^2}{2x^2}=\frac12$, but along axes $f=0$. Limit doesn't exist.

**Conclusion.** $f$ is not continuous, hence not differentiable, though partials exist at $(0,0)$. This shows partial derivatives alone are insufficient for differentiability.

@@@ partial-derivatives-critical-saddle.md
---
title: Saddle point
category: calculus/multivariable/constrained-optimisation
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: saddle point, Hessian, classification
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $f(x,y)=x^2-y^2$ has a saddle point at $(0,0)$.

## Hint

Compute the Hessian: one positive and one negative eigenvalue.

## Solution

**Critical point.** $\nabla f=(2x,-2y)=0\Rightarrow(0,0)$.

**Hessian.** $H=\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&-2\end{pmatrix}$; eigenvalues $2,-2$.

**Behavior.** Along $y=0$: $f=x^2>0$; along $x=0$: $f=-y^2<0$.

**Conclusion.** $(0,0)$ is a saddle point (indefinite Hessian).

@@@ partial-derivatives-euler-homogeneous.md
---
title: Euler's theorem for homogeneous functions
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 2
tags: homogeneous functions, Euler, partial derivatives
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that if $f$ is homogeneous of degree $k$ (i.e. $f(tx,ty)=t^kf(x,y)$), then
$$x f_x+y f_y=k f.$$

## Hint

Differentiate the homogeneity identity with respect to $t$ and evaluate at $t=1$.

## Solution

**Identity.** $f(tx,ty)=t^kf(x,y)$.

**Differentiate.** $\frac{d}{dt}$: $x f_x(tx,ty)+y f_y(tx,ty)=k t^{k-1}f(x,y)$.

**At $t=1$.** $xf_x(x,y)+yf_y(x,y)=kf(x,y)$.

@@@ partial-derivatives-laplacian.md
---
title: The Laplacian
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Laplacian, harmonic functions, second derivatives
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Define the Laplacian $\Delta f$ in $\mathbb{R}^2$ and show that $f(x,y)=x^2-y^2$ is harmonic.

## Hint

$\Delta f=f_{xx}+f_{yy}$.

## Solution

**Definition.** $\Delta f=f_{xx}+f_{yy}$.

**Computation.** $f_{xx}=2$, $f_{yy}=-2$, so $\Delta f=2-2=0$.

**Conclusion.** $f$ is harmonic: $\Delta f=0$.

@@@ partial-derivatives-harmonic-conjugate.md
---
title: Harmonic conjugate
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: m1
difficulty: 5
tags: harmonic conjugate, Cauchy-Riemann, complex
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For $u(x,y)=x^2-y^2$, find a harmonic conjugate $v$ such that $f=u+iv$ satisfies Cauchy–Riemann.

## Hint

Use $u_x=v_y$, $u_y=-v_x$.

## Solution

**CR equations.** $u_x=2x=v_y$, so $v=2xy+g(x)$.

**Second equation.** $u_y=-2y=-v_x=-2y-g'(x)$, so $g'(x)=0$, $g$ constant.

**Result.** $v(x,y)=2xy$.

**Conclusion.** $u+iv=(x+iy)^2=z^2$ is holomorphic, and $v$ is the harmonic conjugate of $u$.

@@@ partial-derivatives-mean-value.md
---
title: Mean value inequality
category: calculus/multivariable/partial-derivatives
level: m1
difficulty: 4
tags: mean value, inequality, gradient
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^n\to\mathbb{R}$ be $C^1$ and $\| \nabla f\|\le M$. Show that $|f(b)-f(a)|\le M\|b-a\|$.

## Hint

Apply the univariate MVT to $t\mapsto f(a+t(b-a))$.

## Solution

**Restriction.** $g(t)=f(a+t(b-a))$ on $[0,1]$. Then $g(0)=f(a)$, $g(1)=f(b)$, and $g'(t)=\nabla f(a+t(b-a))\cdot(b-a)$.

**MVT.** $f(b)-f(a)=g'(c)$ for some $c\in(0,1)$.

**Bound.** $|g'(c)|\le\|\nabla f\|\cdot\|b-a\|\le M\|b-a\|$.

**Conclusion.** Lipschitz bound $|f(b)-f(a)|\le M\|b-a\|$.
@@@ continuity-summary.md
---
title: Summary of continuity
category: calculus/multivariable/continuity
level: l2
difficulty: 2
order: 1
tags: summary, continuity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Summarize the key points of continuity for several variables.

## Hint

Review methods and theorems.

## Solution

**Basic.** Polynomials and quotients of continuous functions (where denominator nonzero) are continuous.

**Continuity at a point.** Show $f(x,y)\to f(x_0,y_0)$; use bounding, polar coordinates, or the squeeze theorem.

**Discontinuity.** Exhibit two paths with different limits, or a path where the limit diverges or doesn't exist.

**Notable counterexamples.** $f(x,y)=\frac{x}{x+y}$ at origin, $f(x,y) = \sin(1/(x^2+y^2))$ at origin.


**Key theorems.** Continuous image of compact is compact; continuous on compact is uniformly continuous (Heine–Cantor); norm and projections are continuous.

@@@ continuity-simple-poly.md
---
title: Continuity of a polynomial
category: calculus/multivariable/continuity
level: lycee
difficulty: 1
tags: continuity, polynomial, continuity everywhere
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $f(x,y)=x^2+3xy-y^2+2x-1$ is continuous on $\mathbb{R}^2$.

## Hint

Polynomials in several variables are continuous (sums and products of continuous coordinate projections).

## Solution

**Continuity of polynomials.** The functions $(x,y)\mapsto x$ and $(x,y)\mapsto y$ are continuous, and sums/products of continuous functions are continuous.

**Conclusion.** $f$ is continuous on $\mathbb{R}^2$ by the composition rules.

## Statement

Where is $f(x,y)=\dfrac{x+2y}{x^2+y^2+1}$ continuous?

## Hint

A quotient of continuous functions is continuous wherever the denominator is nonzero.

## Solution

**Denominator.** $x^2+y^2+1\neq0$ for every $(x,y) \in \mathbb{R}^2$.

**Continuity.** $f$ is continuous on $\mathbb{R}^2$.

@@@ continuity-polar-trick.md
---
title: Continuity via polar coordinates
category: calculus/multivariable/continuity
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: polar, continuity, bound
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $f(x,y)=\dfrac{x^3+y^3}{x^2+y^2}$ (with $f(0,0)=0$) is continuous at $(0,0)$.

## Hint

Use Polar coordinates. 

## Solution

**Polar coordinates.** For $(x,y) = (r\cos \theta, r \sin \theta)$, 
$$ |f(x,y)|  =r|\cos^3\theta+\sin^3\theta| \leq 2r = 2 \sqrt{x^2 + y^2} \rightarrow 0 $$
as $(x,y) \rightarrow (0,0)$. 

**Conclusion.** $\lim_{(x,y)\to(0,0)}f=0=f(0,0)$: $f$ is continuous at the origin.

@@@ continuity-discontinuous-two-paths.md
---
title: Discontinuity via two paths
category: calculus/multivariable/continuity
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: discontinuity, paths, limits
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the functions 
$$ f(x,y)=\dfrac{x}{x+y} \, \, \text{and} \, \, g(x,y)=\dfrac{xy}{x+y} $$ 
with $f(0,0)= g(0,0)=0$ are not continuous at the origin.

## Hint

Check the paths $y=ax$ and $y = ax + b x^2$.

## Solution

**Continuity of $f$.** On $y=ax$, $f(x,ax)=\frac{1}{1+a}$. As $x\to0$, this tends to $\frac{1}{1+a}$ for each $a \neq -1$. Hence $f$ is not continuous at the origin. 
\par
**Continuity of $g$.** On $y=ax$, $g(x,ax)=\frac{ax}{1+a} \rightarrow 0$ as $x\to0$ for any $a \neq -1$ (this does not mean $g$ is continuous!). Along $y=ax+bx^2$ with $a=-1$, $b\neq0$: 
$$ g(x,-x+bx^2)=\frac{x(-x+bx^2)}{bx^2}=\frac{-x+bx^2}{bx}=\frac{-1}{b}+x \rightarrow \frac{-1}{b} $$ 
as $x \rightarrow 0$. Limits differ along different paths; $g$ is not continuous at the origin.

**Remark.** The example of $g$ shows that checking limits along all straight lines through the origin is necessary but *not* sufficient for the existence of a two-variable limit.

@@@ continuity-radial-dependence.md
---
title: A function that is not continuous despite radial limits
category: calculus/multivariable/continuity
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: continuity, radial limit, counterexample
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that $f(x,y)=\dfrac{x^2y}{x^4+y^2}$ (with $f(0,0)=0$) is not continuous at $(0,0)$, even though $f(x,ax)\to0$ for every $a$.

## Hint

Test the path $y=x^2$.

## Solution

**Radial limits.** $f(x,ax)=\frac{ax^3}{x^4+a^2x^2}=\frac{ax}{x^2+a^2}\to0$ for fixed $a$.

**Parabolic path.** $f(x,x^2)=\frac{x^4}{2x^4}=\frac12\not\to0$.

**Conclusion.** $f$ is not continuous at the origin despite radial limits being zero.

@@@ continuity-piecewise-xy.md

title: Continuity of a piecewise function

Show that $f(x,y)=\frac{xy}{\sqrt{x^2+y^2}}$ (with $f(0,0)=0$) is continuous at $(0,0)$.

## Hint

$|xy|\le\frac12(x^2+y^2)$.

## Solution

**Bound.** $|f(x,y)| = \frac{|xy|}{\sqrt{x^2+y^2}} \le\frac12\sqrt{x^2+y^2}\to0$.
**Conclusion.**  $f$  is continuous at $(0,0)$.

@@@ continuity-discontinuous-bounded.md

## Statement

Show that the function
$$f(x,y)=\sin\!\left(\frac{1}{x^2+y^2}\right) \, \text{for} \, (x,y)\neq(0,0), \, \, f(0,0)=0 $$
is not continuous at the origin.
